#7409. What DoorDash’s Instacart acquisition says about the modern delivery platform pitch

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This story was first published by Digiday sibling Modern Retail.

These days, delivery platforms have to offer more than just delivery services to brands and retailers.

DoorDash’s announced plans to acquire Instacart for $17 billion point to the constant need to gain market share by diversifying services and offerings.

Over the years, both DoorDash and Instacart have invested heavily in new services like AI assistants, advertising programs and in-store B-to-B solutions. DoorDash, in particular, has been on an expansion streak, acquiring U.K.-based competitor Deliveroo and restaurant reservations platform SevenRooms in 2025. Last year, DoorDash also acquired search ad startup Symbiosys for $175 million to deepen its advertising capabilities.

With the high cost of last-mile delivery, companies like DoorDash and Instacart have expanded well beyond delivering groceries and takeout.

Leah Sallen, U.S. head of CPG and retail strategy at programmatic ad tech platform MiQ, said the growing services and tools DoorDash has come to provide have made it more than just a marketplace for delivery. “Between delivery, grocery shopping, restaurant reservations and advertising, DoorDash is gaining access to more consumer touchpoints and insights into when, where and how people shop,” she said.

Over the past few years, DoorDash has aggressively built out its non-restaurant marketplace, partnering with major grocery, convenience and retail brands like Ulta, Skims and Macy’s. The company also introduced logistics features to solve pain points, like Dasher Returns. On the technology side, DoorDash has rolled out AI-powered tools, such as Ask DoorDash for shopping recommendations, DashBuddy for courier support and DashOS for restaurant customer management. These solutions are meant to create an all-in-one ecosystem for B-to-B infrastructure and logistics streamlining.

Now, the Instacart consolidation will bring DoorDash grocery retailers that the platform currently does not offer its customers, notably Walmart and Sam’s Club. But with that market share also comes challenges in maintaining retail relationships by offering more than just delivery solutions.

Neil Saunders, managing director of GlobalData, said DoorDash’s latest hefty acquisition will require further streamlining to make sense as a long-term investment.

Saunders said Instacart’s operations still largely rely on workers manually picking groceries. He said this is not only labor-intensive, but it’s also harder to scale efficiently as digital grocery orders continue to grow. “Either way, DoorDash should aim to position itself as a technology and infrastructure partner rather than simply a delivery provider,” Saunders said.

Some experts believe DoorDash’s proactive strategy is to simply keep up with Amazon’s delivery offerings. However, retail media networks have also become an important revenue source for delivery platforms in recent years.

At its surface, the DoorDash-Instacart deal is about two massive last-mile companies consolidating. But for advertisers, it could mean a broader disruption in retail loyalty, said Sallen, as the two platforms’ merger brings together deep data on restaurant, grocery and retail shopping.

“We have already seen this play out with TikTok Shop, which fundamentally changed consumer shopping behavior by using sophisticated algorithms to deliver highly personalized product recommendations,” Sallen said. A similar shift can happen with this acquisition, Sallen said, thanks to the combination of DoorDash’s immediate-consumption insights and Instacart’s long-standing grocery data.

Sallen explained that, similar to how Amazon became a starting point for shopping, consumers may increasingly turn to platforms like DoorDash to find the best product, price or delivery option before deciding which retailer to purchase from. In turn, she said, “That shift from selecting the retailer first to selecting the retailer last could prompt CPG brands to reconsider how they allocate their retail media dollars.”

Matt Gilbert, CEO of marketing platform Partnerize, agreed that the significance of a DoorDash and Instacart combination goes well beyond delivery.

“It’s about the growing concentration of consumer discovery, advertising, purchasing decisions and transaction data within a single commerce ecosystem,” Gilbert said. For consumers, that could mean greater convenience and fewer steps between discovering a product and buying it. “For advertisers, it creates an opportunity to connect marketing investment more directly to purchasing behavior across more categories and occasions.”

On the other hand, Gilbert said that when platforms increasingly control the consumer experience, the transaction and the reporting of advertising performance, advertisers need assurance that they’re measuring genuine incremental influence — “and not simply taking credit for purchases that would have happened anyway,” said Gilbert.

Sallen added that, as platforms like DoorDash add capabilities and AI increasingly influences shopping decisions, “The consumer path to purchase is becoming more diluted and harder for advertisers to follow.”

That shift could become even more pronounced as AI agents increasingly help consumers weigh price, proximity and product availability before deciding what and where to buy. Sallen said this could mean delivery behemoths like DoorDash could have an advantage in shaping AI-driven recommendations to millions of consumers. “The more intelligence it [DoorDash] can bring together, the more personalized it can make the shopping experience,” Sallen said.

“DoorDash’s growing portfolio of services could position it as a one-stop shop for retailers, with data becoming one of its biggest advantages,” Sallen added.

Publishers are pushing back against Meta’s AI scraping terms, testing their leverage over a platform they still rely on.

Political and news creators are relied on now more than ever, but what platforms are working for them? Which ones aren’t?

#7403. Digiday+ Research 2026 Creator Marketing Index: A brand guide to social media campaigns

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This research is based on unique data collected from our proprietary audience of publisher, agency, brand and tech insiders. It’s available to Digiday+ members. More from the series →

As creator marketing becomes a fixture in many brands’ marketing strategies, creators’ roles are shifting and expanding. Brands may pay creators to produce sponsored content, gift products to them in the hope of organic posts, or rely on a combination of creator- and brand-produced content published on their own social media accounts.

Overall, social media remains the leading channel for creator partnerships: 96% of respondents to Digiday’s first-quarter survey of brands and agencies said they partner with creators to create social media content. Within social media, Instagram (90%), Facebook (80%), YouTube (72%) and TikTok (63%) were the platforms marketers said they used most.

Across these social platforms, brands are taking various approaches to creator campaigns, from sponsored and gifted posts to content formats like product demonstrations, styled photos and event coverage. Understanding how these approaches perform can help brands make informed decisions about creator partnerships, content formats and campaign investment.

To measure the impact creator content has on brand campaigns, Digiday+ Research analyzed the engagement levels on leading creators’ social media campaigns from January to September 2026.

The Digiday Creator Marketing Index collected data from 1,755 social media posts and 20 branded social media campaigns, analyzing the top posts associated with each campaign’s hashtag and scoring them across a set of key dimensions to create a total index average score. Results are dependent upon the list of social media campaigns and the time period of data collection, generating a snapshot of the creator marketing landscape at a specific moment in time.

Digiday’s Index collected social media data from January to September 2026. Due to data restrictions, TikTok views were not captured. Data collection was conducted by Dash Social, an agentic social platform powered by brand-intelligent AI.

The social media campaigns were selected by Digiday’s editorial team.

When considering video content performance by platform, Instagram Reels received far more video views than YouTube’s Shorts and long-form videos, while TikTok videos had higher average engagement in later analysis. However, these results should be considered within the context of the Index’s focus on branded social media campaigns, meaning that the majority of posts included marketing-focused content rather than non-brand-related content, which performs the best on YouTube. Additionally, on YouTube — a video-first platform with a core strength in long-form content — most of the captured posts consisted of Shorts rather than long-form posts.

Average engagement rate — engagement divided by follower count — was only measurable on Instagram. When looking at average engagement rate by format type on Instagram, Reels performed the best among Instagram’s formats at an 11% engagement rate, underscoring the strength of short-form video on Instagram. Still posts came in second with a 7% engagement rate, followed by Carousel posts at 5%. Carousel posts can contain videos, but views were not counted as part of the average engagement rate for Carousel posts with video. Likewise, views were not counted as part of the average engagement rate for Still posts or Reels.

The top 75 posts associated with each campaign’s hashtag were identified based on overall engagement, including likes and comments across platforms. When Digiday analyzed those top-performing posts by platform, with engagement weighted equally across platforms, Instagram accounted for the largest share of posts associated with campaign hashtags.

However, TikTok posts within the top 75 generated higher average engagement than posts on other platforms. In other words, Instagram appeared more frequently among the highest-performing posts overall, while the top-performing TikTok posts generated greater engagement on average.

According to Digiday’s analysis, Instagram is a more consistent platform for performance, while TikTok has greater reach potential and a higher engagement ceiling. For brands running longer campaigns or prioritizing consistency, Instagram offers a more predictable environment for creator marketing. For smaller brands or those with limited budgets, TikTok is likely the better platform choice for creator marketing. While engagement on TikTok isn’t as consistent, its higher engagement ceiling gives creator content a better possibility of reaching a wider audience.

Looking more closely at sponsored and gifted posts, the Index defines them as follows. Sponsored posts refer to content created through a direct partnership between a brand and a creator. Gifted posts feature products that brands have sent to creators, typically as part of a PR effort, without a paid content agreement. Digiday classified posts as sponsored or gifted based on disclosures in captions, including paid labels, disclaimers, and hashtags such as #ad, #paid, #gifted and #PR. In some video posts, creators verbally expressed whether they had been paid by a brand.

The most common content types across both sponsored posts and gifted posts were product demonstrations and style shots. The Index defines a demo as content about a single brand or product that includes a description and shows a person using the item. A style shot is content that showcases a product in a scenic or visually styled setting without an explanation of how to use the item.

Style shots appeared more frequently in gifted posts than they did in sponsored posts, by a ratio of 19 to 15, indicating that creators favor that format when featuring products they have received for free without a formal content agreement. One possible explanation is that demos can appear to be overtly promotional since they feature one product as opposed to a “get ready with me” (GRWM) format that typically showcases multiple brands. Creators may want to avoid producing content that could be interpreted as advertising when it comes to gifted items, according to Digiday’s analysis.

When comparing average engagement rates, sponsored posts outperformed gifted posts across content types, with one notable exception: demos  — the most used content format type — where gifted posts performed best. Style shots displayed the opposite pattern, performing better in sponsored content where they also delivered the highest overall engagement among the content formats analyzed.

One potential factor behind this difference is the level of production and associated costs behind the content. Gifted style shots were often simple flat lays or product images, while sponsored style shots frequently included product videos. Brands often give creators larger budgets to develop content for sponsored product posts. That distinction is particularly important for product categories like fragrance, where brands rely on visual storytelling to communicate abstract qualities like mood and sensory experience.

While engagement provides a strong measure of how audiences respond to creator content, reach — calculated by average views — offers another important metric of performance. Digiday’s analysis found that sponsored posts had greater overall reach based on views than gifted posts, by a margin of 334,055 to 48,008. Within sponsored posts, style shots (707,646), demos (250,764) and influencer events (231,595) had the most views. Among gifted posts, demos generated the most views (81,023), well below sponsored formats.

That margin of difference is likely also linked to budget differences, according to Digiday’s analysis. Brands typically provide creators with paid media budgets to amplify sponsored posts beyond a creator’s existing audience, while gifted posts are limited to a creator’s own audience. As a result, sponsored content can generate both organic and paid reach, while gifted product content relies on organic distribution with more limited reach.

This Index exclusively analyzes brand-generated hashtags that incorporated either a brand name or a trademarked product name, such as #exaggereyes, #chocolatesoleil and #hydroboost.

The majority of the highest-ranked campaigns had a strong social media presence with a significant number of brand posts on the brand-owned handle, in addition to creator posts. By contrast, lower-ranked campaigns had a weaker presence on brand-owned channels, with no brand-generated posts in many cases. These findings suggest that while creator participation is an important driver of campaign success, brands’ own social media activity also plays a critical role, according to Digiday’s analysis.

Many of the highest-ranked campaigns also included a mix of gifted posts and sponsored posts. Gifted posts came from both macro- and micro-creators, indicating that high-ranked campaigns sent products to a wide range of content creators. However, investment was another important factor. All of the highest-ranked campaigns had large budgets backing them, highlighting the growing role of pay-to-play social media campaigns, where brands must invest in both creator partnerships and amplification to build reach.

Not all campaigns with paid support performed equally, however, suggesting that investment alone does not guarantee stronger performance.

Charlotte Tilbury’s #exaggereyes campaign ranked No. 1 in Digiday’s Index. One of its defining characteristics was the use of gifted posts: 35% of the campaign’s posts came from creators who received gifted products, the highest share among the campaigns Digiday analyzed.

Charlotte Tilbury’s collaboration with Influenster, a product sampling and review platform owned by Bazaarvoice, generated a large portion of the gifted posts for the #exaggereyes campaign. Rather than selecting creators and sending them PR packages directly, the brand relied on Influenster to collect user-generated content for its own use. Most of the gifted posts were demo videos, a format that Digiday’s analysis found generated higher average engagement within gifted posts than sponsored posts.

Neutrogena’s #hydroboost campaign ranked No. 2 in the Index and focused on a product-line extension. Neutrogena heavily invested in sending gifted products and directly collaborating with creators. As a result, the Hydro Boost campaign had the highest share of brand-seeded content — sponsored or gifted posts — at 39% of all posts.

The campaign also incorporated the broader #hyaluronicacid hashtag alongside the branded #hyrdoboost hashtag. This strategy allowed Neutrogena to not only position the Hydro Boost product as a line extension but also connect the campaign to the larger hyaluronic acid product category, potentially exposing the campaign to audiences with a broader interest in hyaluronic acid products.

Like Neutrogena’s campaign, Too Faced’s #chocolatesoleil campaign promoted a product-line extension but with a focus on the Brazilian market. Thirty-four of the top 75 posts associated with the Chocolate Soleil campaign hashtag mentioned Brazil, including content from Too Faced’s Brazilian social media account and other Brazilian creator accounts, indicating a deliberate focus on localizing the campaign for that market.

The campaign also had the lowest share of sponsored or gifted posts among the 10 highest-ranked campaigns, with 74 of its top 75 posts classified as organic posts. Instead, the campaign focused heavily on the product launch and activity generated through Too Faced’s brand handle to entice creators to test the product and create content about it.

Taken together, these campaigns show that there is no single formula for a successful brand-generated hashtag campaign. High-performing campaigns combined product seeding, creator partnerships, brand-owned content, organic activity and localized strategies in different ways. According to Digiday’s analysis, brand-generated hashtags can give brands greater ownership of the campaign conversation, particularly when tied to a specific product or launch, but their narrower focus may limit the volume and variety of content generated around them.

Making ads people want to watch is now a strategy. SharkNinja’s celebrity filled holiday spot shows why.

Google search was the line item buyers never questioned. That’s changing.

Customers who use Alexa for Shopping are spending 50% more than those who don’t.

#7388. FinOps Data Engineer at Supabase

About the RoleWe're looking for a FinOps Data Engineer to help drive cost optimization and cloud financial governance across our AWS infrastructure and GCP footprint. You'll work cross-functionally with Engineering, Finance, and Product to find and ship savings, build the tooling necessary to maximize efficiency of every dollar spent in the cloud, and cultivate a culture of cost awareness across engineering teams. This role owns the data side of that mission: the models, pipelines, and analysis that make cost, usage, and margin numbers trustworthy across the company.This is a hands-on engineering role, not an analyst seat. You'll do the schlep work other teams don't have time for, digging into a bucket's access pattern, writing the dbt model or Python pipeline that ships the fix, building the cost-vs-revenue mart that answers a margin question once instead of every time, and chasing a number until it reconciles. Strong communication matters as much as technical depth, you'll regularly explain a savings case or a data model's implications to an engineering team, a finance stakeholder, and a leadership audience in the same week.What You'll Be Responsible ForIn this role, you'll:

Find and ship savings across AWS and GCP: Analyze billing, usage, and cost trends to identify opportunities, rightsizing, storage lifecycle policies, data transfer, and spot/auto-scaling. Take ideas from identification through to a realized dollar figure, not just a recommendation.

Build and maintain the FinOps data pipeline: Design and run the BigQuery/dbt models that turn raw billing and usage data (CUR, Storage Lens) into reconciled, trustworthy mart tables, the foundation everything else in FinOps is built on.

Build cost-vs-revenue and customer segmentation models: Model unit economics and margin by product line and customer segment, and extend coverage as new products and segments come online.

Apply data science to cost and usage data: Build forecasting models, detect anomalies statistically rather than by threshold alone, and quantify savings opportunities (e.g. storage lifecycle, request-cost patterns) with rigor a spreadsheet can't match.

Build and maintain cost attribution: Tagging policies, cost allocation, and chargeback/showback so engineering teams can see and own their own spend. This is foundational work other savings work depends on.

Automate cost governance: Build automated cost reporting, anomaly detection, and alerting. Write the scripts and infrastructure-as-code (Python, Terraform, Lambda) that enforce cost policy rather than relying on manual review.

Partner with engineering on architecture decisions: Bring cost into the room early, cost-efficiencies are cheaper to get right at design than to retrofit.

Work with Finance on forecasting and reporting: Support budget tracking and financial KPIs; build dashboards and self-serve analytics engineering and finance can both read.

Have strong AWS expertise and working GCP knowledge, including pricing models, billing structures, and cost optimization mechanics.

Have data engineering depth: dbt, BigQuery (or an equivalent modern warehouse), SQL at scale, and data modeling for analytics (dimensional modeling, incremental models, testing).

Have data science fundamentals: statistical forecasting, anomaly detection, and experimentation, and know when a rigorous model beats a rule of thumb.

Have automation and scripting skills: Python, Terraform/ pulumi, Lambda, or Bash.

Are comfortable with cost/billing tooling: AWS Cost Explorer, Cost & Usage Reports, GCP Billing Reports, and BI/dashboarding (we use Hex).

Have experience with, or are comfortable picking up, Linear and Notion as the system of record for tracking and documenting FinOps work.

Experience with a dedicated cloud cost management platform (e.g. CloudZero, Vantage, or similar) is a plus, not required.

Can analyze cost data and present it clearly to both technical and non-technical audiences.

Have prior experience spanning data analytics, data engineering, or data science in a FinOps, cloud cost management, or cloud governance context.

Are energized by doing the unglamorous, high-leverage work (the schlep) that unblocks other teams, and by working cross-functionally without positional authority.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

Once a year, the entire company gathers in a new city for a week of connection, collaboration, and fun. It’s a highlight of our year.

We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

Over $1B raised (including our $500M Series F)

We move fast, build in public, and use what we ship. If it’s in your project, we probably use it in ours too. We believe deeply in the open-source ecosystem and strive to support—not replace—existing tools and communities.

#7379. #AskNewser: ABC, Fox News and NewsNation Prepare for an Unpredictable Midterm Election Night

Interest in the 2026 midterm elections is expected to be sky-high, and news outlets are pulling out all the stops to meet the demand, while covering every angle.

With polls suggesting a possible blue wave, networks are preparing for that outcome, which could signal a referendum on Donald Trump’s presidency.

However, if the results differ from polling expectations and Republicans retain control of one chamber of Congress or don’t suffer the anticipated deep losses, these networks will have to adapt, too.

As part of the return of TVNewser’s AskNewser series, we spoke to three news executives ahead of election day: ABC News’ political director, Averi Harper; Fox News, evp, Washington news and politics, Douglas Rohrbeck; and NewsNation’s political editor, Chris Stirewalt, about their preparedness, the uniqueness of this consequential election, and the tech they want to showcase on election night, among other things.

ADWEEK: What makes covering this year’s midterm elections different from other elections, especially presidential elections?

Averi Harper: Midterms are often viewed as a referendum on the president and the party in power. This year, we’re examining how issues like affordability, immigration, and the war with Iran are influencing voters’ decisions.

Unlike a presidential election, where so much attention is focused on the two candidates atop the ticket, midterm election coverage requires us to connect the dots. It requires us to understand what’s happening in individual communities and place those developments within the broader national political environment.

Douglas Rohrbeck: Midterm elections present a different challenge because there isn’t one presidential race driving the entire national conversation. Our job is to connect those stories and make a complicated election night easy to follow. This year, we’re bringing together our extensive team of journalists, the Fox News Decision Desk, and new technology across television, streaming, and digital to deliver a comprehensive picture of what’s happening nationwide.

Chris Stirewalt: Midterm elections have much lower turnout than presidential elections, and figuring out winners and losers depends as much on who isn’t voting as on how people are voting. So missing voters are just as much a part of the story as the ones who show up.

AW: What do you think is the big unknown that news outlets should anticipate on election night?

Harper: The biggest unknown is the composition of the electorate— who shows up to the polls and who skips out.  We saw significant shifts among key voting blocs in 2024, including Latino voters and younger voters.

We’re watching whether the enthusiasm we’ve seen among Democratic voters translates into turnout in battlegrounds and whether Republicans can mobilize their coalition without President Trump himself on the ballot.

Rohrbeck: The biggest unknown is always how the results will unfold and where the story will take us. That’s why flexibility is so important. We have correspondents positioned across the country, a seasoned anchor team, and the production capabilities to pivot quickly as developments warrant. Our priority is to be accurate, provide context, and make sure viewers understand what’s happening even as things are still developing.

Stirewalt: We know the polls will be wrong, but we don’t know by how much or in what direction. And it behooves all of us to come into the final day of the election with enough humility to say that our assumptions may or may not be true. It’s being open to the possibility that Democrats may be doing better than expected, that Republicans may again be doing better than polling suggested, or that polls are right on the money.

AW: How are you going to differentiate yourselves from your competitors?

Harper: We’ve invested heavily in reporting from communities across the country in the run-up to elections, and we’ll have a dozen teams deployed in key states on election night. We’re combining that reporting with an unprecedented amount of data to provide a fuller picture of the electorate. Our goal is to make a complicated election night accessible, informative, and meaningful to viewers, regardless of where they’re watching.

Rohrbeck: On election night, Bret Baier and Martha MacCallum bring the experience, credibility, and perspective viewers have come to expect from us. We no longer think of election night as a television broadcast that gets repurposed elsewhere. One newsgathering operation is powering distinct experiences across television, streaming, digital, audio, and social, allowing audiences to engage with our reporting wherever and however they choose.

Stirewalt: The crucial elements are independence and transparency. We are taking an NFL RedZone-style approach: we’ll follow the voters and the races as they unfold with our election data partners at Decision Desk HQ. We’re going to focus on giving people correct calls quickly and providing the context for the races that haven’t been called yet about what will be necessary to make those calls. So, I would say we’re going to have a transparent process, and we’re going to have independent race calling so that there’s no funny business.

AW: What new technology will you incorporate into your election night coverage?

Harper: I’m particularly excited about two new tools that will help us tell the story of this election in greater depth. The first is our new Voter Poll, which will give us data from every state with a Senate or gubernatorial race — 45 statewide polls in total. Our national sample will also be roughly three times the size of the 2024 National Exit Poll. That gives us an extraordinary opportunity to understand what motivated voters and how different communities cast their ballots.

We’re also introducing Vote Shift, which will allow viewers to see in real time how counties and states are voting compared with the 2024 presidential election. And we’ll be bringing all of this to viewers from our redesigned election headquarters, featuring two studios and more than 151 million LED pixels.

Rohrbeck: We’re making a significant investment in augmented reality, data-driven graphics, and artificial intelligence to make election coverage more informative, interactive, and accessible. On television, enhanced AR technology will let us visualize county-level voting shifts, track the path to control of Congress, and bring complex election data to life in real time. On the Fox News App, we’re introducing an AI-powered election chatbot that allows users to ask questions about individual races and results and receive answers drawn directly from Fox News reporting. Combined with interactive maps and new audience engagement features, these innovations reflect how we’re thinking about the future of news consumption.

Stirewalt: Connell McShane will be the Pat Summerall to my John Madden on election night. We have an overhauled studio in New York with great new displays, even utilizing the floor panels, so that we’ll be able to do all kinds of data visualization to talk about the calls and what’s coming next. I think it’s going to be a lot of fun, and it looks fantastic.

AW: When do you anticipate viewers will know who controls the Senate and the House?

Harper: Control of both chambers could come down to a handful of extremely competitive races, and some of those states have lengthy vote-counting processes. Our priority is accuracy over speed. We want viewers to understand not just the results, but why some races can be projected quickly while others will require patience.

Rohrbeck: Our Fox News Decision Desk will be analyzing results throughout the evening, and we’ll make projections when the data supports them. Ultimately, accuracy will always take precedence over speed. Our responsibility is to give viewers the most reliable information possible.

Stirewalt: If Democrats have been understated or if Republicans have been understated substantially, we’ll know early because we’ll see, even in the 7 o’clock hour, some strong indications about what’s going on. It’ll be too soon to say by then, but I can imagine the call on the House will come in the 9 o’clock hour, and then, depending on how close the Senate is, it could be in the wee hours, or it could be in a week. We’ll see!

Mark Mwachiro is a TVNewser contributor who has been writing for the blog since March 2022. Based out of New York, Mark has also contributed to ADWEEK.

Select ADWEEK as one of your favorite sources and stay up to date on our latest content.

#7375. Site Reliability Engineer at Supabase

Supabase manages millions of Postgres instances and is growing. We have strong teams across observability, release engineering, and incident management — and we're concentrating our reliability efforts into a dedicated SRE practice that ties the discipline together across the platform.

You'll be embedded within Service Operations, and your primary job is to make every engineering team more reliable — not by owning their infrastructure, but by establishing the practices, frameworks, and feedback loops that let them own reliability themselves. You'll work across the org: sometimes setting the standard, sometimes pair-programming a fix, sometimes helping a team define their error budget, sometimes telling them it's exhausted.

This role is ideal for someone who has a strong vision for how SRE should work and thrives in async, fast-paced environments where influence matters more than authority.

Partner with service teams to define meaningful SLIs and SLOs grounded in customer experience, and build the error budget policies that turn them into engineering decisions

Own and evolve the Operational Readiness Review (ORR) process — conducting reviews for new services and major changes across observability, alerting, runbooks, capacity, and graceful degradation

Strengthen the incident-to-improvement pipeline: connecting postmortem findings to operational readiness gaps, identifying repeat failure patterns, and driving systemic fixes

Act as the reliability expert teams pull in for architecture reviews, failure mode analysis, dependency mapping, and resilience design

Identify and quantify operational toil across the org, and build or advocate for automation that eliminates it

Help teams design sustainable on-call practices: alert quality, escalation paths, runbook coverage, and noise reduction

Track and report on org-wide operational maturity, surfacing systemic gaps and driving remediation

Have 7+ years of experience in SRE, production engineering, or reliability-focused roles, including experience shaping SRE practices and driving adoption across engineering teams

Have a software engineering mindset — you write code and build tools, not just configure them

Have hands-on experience defining and operationalizing SLOs/SLIs at scale, including error budget policies that actually influenced engineering decisions

Have deep experience with incident response, postmortem facilitation, and turning incident learnings into systemic improvements

Have worked with large-scale multi-tenant systems (bonus: managed database platforms or Postgres)

Are proficient with cloud infrastructure (AWS preferred) and infrastructure-as-code (Pulumi preferred, Terraform/CDK also acceptable)

Communicate clearly and persuasively — this role requires influencing without authority across a distributed org

Have experience in async or globally distributed teams

Experience with Kubernetes-based platform operations

The load-bearing questions are open and there are few set policies and processes.

You will be the technical authority on revenue. Author our revenue accounting policy set — principal versus agent on brokered inference, breakage on expiring credits, performance obligation identification and allocation, minimum commitment versus overage, and contract modifications — and make each one stand up to an external auditor

Own the monthly revenue close: recognition, the deferred revenue rollforward, contract asset and liability balances, and the flux analysis

Own the revenue portion of our external audit, from the PBC list through the technical positions

Replace manual revenue entries with an automated billing-to-revenue reconciliation and a documented exception process — revenue should be computed by a system and reviewed by a person, with invoice accuracy above 99% and root-cause tracking on the misses

Govern order to cash: billing accuracy, dispute resolution, and an AR and collections process with real DSO and aging targets

Own commission capitalization and amortization under ASC 340-40

Review non-standard deal structures before they're signed, so the accounting consequence is a decision rather than a discovery

You're a licensed CPA with a Big 4 or comparable technical accounting foundation, plus real industry experience in a high-growth startup environment

You have demonstrable ASC 606 experience with usage-based or consumption revenue

You write technical memos that a reviewer can follow without a meeting: facts, guidance, analysis, conclusion

You've owned a monthly revenue close hands-on, and you've closed revenue through at least one external audit

You're comfortable being the technical authority without a bench behind you

Enough SQL to validate a recognition schedule against raw usage data yourself

#7354. Shopify Stores Face Suspected AI-Driven Attacks

German-based software engineer Roman Zipp was buying a mechanical keyboard from a Shopify-powered storefront last month, when he discovered a series of ecommerce cyberattacks.

As he checked out, he noticed the URL was from a domain affiliated neither with Shopify nor the merchant, he told ADWEEK.

Examining the source code revealed a redirect to a fake checkout screen, likely meant to fool unsuspecting shoppers into handing over their personal information.

The code wasn’t in the Shopify platform itself, but a third-party extension used by the merchant via the Shopify App Store — a marketplace where sellers can find apps to add functionality to their storefronts, like recommendation engines or post-checkout upsell screens.

Mitchell Parton is ADWEEK's senior commerce reporter, covering retail media as well as the rise of agentic and social commerce. Before joining ADWEEK in 2026, he was a reporter at Digiday Media's Modern Retail and before that wrote for several regional publications in Texas.

#7350. SDK Engineer - Python at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We're looking for a SDK Engineer - Python to join our SDK team and own how developers talk to Supabase from Python. You'll work closely with the rest of the SDK team, our Auth, Realtime, Storage, PostgREST, Functions, CLI and Docs teams, and the open-source community, helping us make the Python SDK a library Python developers genuinely enjoy using.

Python developers reach Supabase from backend services, data pipelines, notebooks, and a growing number of AI agents and agent frameworks. The Python SDK is how all of them talk to the platform: database queries, auth, storage, realtime and edge functions, in both sync and async flavors.

This is a library-authoring role, and the work happens in the open: public repos, public issue trackers, and API decisions whose consequences last for years. If you get satisfaction from a type checker that catches the bug before your users do, from an async client that cleans up after itself, and from an issue tracker that isn't a graveyard, you'll like it here.

This role is ideal for someone who thrives in async, fast-paced environments and is excited about building developer tools that scale to millions.

Build and evolve our Python SDK across database, auth, storage, realtime and functions, with both sync and async APIs that feel natural in Python.

Start new things. Alongside the maintenance work, find what's missing, make the case for it, and build it. Nobody will hand you the list.

Work in the open. Triage inbound issues, review and shepherd outside contributions, keep CI and release automation healthy, and hold a clear, kind line on scope.

Own the typing experience. Make the SDK fully typed and checker-friendly, so autocomplete and static analysis stay correct in real codebases.

Keep us honest across environments. Multiple Python versions, sync and async frameworks, workers, serverless and notebooks — with dependency ranges that don't fight our users' projects. Test it, don't assume it.

Work on realtime. Long-lived websocket connections, reconnection, and subscription and presence semantics.

Track the platform. When auth, realtime, storage, postgrest or edge functions ship a change, make sure the SDK reflects it, and that we say so in the changelog.

Design in public. Write the RFCs, migration guides, release notes and docs. Treat a breaking change as a deprecation path plus a migration story, not a version bump.

Make our SDK legible to AI coding agents, which are now a major consumer of it.

Know modern Python typing deeply. You use generics, protocols, overloads and TypedDict as design tools, run strict type checking, and fix type errors without reaching for Any.

Have authored or maintained a published library with a public API and users outside your own company. You know what it costs to change something people depend on.

Know the maintainer side of open source. Issue triage at volume, reviewing outside PRs, release automation, changelogs and semver, security reports, and saying no to a feature request without losing the person who asked.

Are fluent in asyncio and have opinions on how a library should serve both sync and async callers.

Know Python packaging for libraries: pyproject.toml, wheels, modern tooling, version support policy, and why libraries shouldn't pin their dependencies tightly.

Test as a habit: unit, type-level and end-to-end against a real stack. Every fix comes with a regression test.

Write well. Design docs, changelogs and issue replies are a large part of the output here, much of it public, and we're async by default.

Have real autonomy: you find the important work, scope it, ship it, and say what you didn't do.

Communicate clearly across both technical and non-technical audiences

Have experience in async or globally distributed teams

Are energized by solving real-world problems for developers

Are comfortable navigating ambiguity and moving quickly

Bonus points if you: have run a major-version migration for a public package, know your way around JWT/OAuth, have built integrations for AI agent frameworks, or have generated idiomatic clients from a schema.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

Once a year, the entire company gathers in a new city for a week of connection, collaboration, and fun. It’s a highlight of our year.

We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

#7347. The Trade Desk Brings Back Dave Pickles Amid Growing Pains

Three years after he stepped away, Dave Pickles is coming back to The Trade Desk amid the worst stretch in its history as a public company.

The co-founder and former chief technology officer, who left his role and the company’s board in 2023, will establish and lead a new CTO Council, The Trade Desk announced today. He has stayed on as advisor since leaving.

The council will work with The Trade Desk’s engineering and product teams on its core technology and product roadmap. The company, in a blog post, said that work includes “architectural direction, operational excellence, and key engineering investments—including agentic capabilities and advanced AI-powered advertising workflows.”

“I’m so excited Dave is back. I’ve missed working with him,” CEO Jeff Green said in a statement. “We’ve had so many successes together over the years, and I’m energized to take all of that history and point it toward the future. This is a pivotal crossroads for our company and the industry.”

The announcement lands a week before The Trade Desk is expected to report third-quarter earnings on Nov. 9.

The Trade Desk went public in 2016, hitting $1.1 billion market capitalization on its first day, betting the open web would hold against the walled gardens. Pickles ran the company’s technology through most of the climb that followed.

The company he is rejoining has slowing revenue growth, partners pushing back on its products, and a stock sitting 91% below its peak.

In August, TTD reported second-quarter revenue of $715 million, up 3% year-over-year but about $37.6 million short of analysts’ forecast. It guided third-quarter revenue to at least $650 million, roughly 12% below the same quarter a year earlier, which would be its first YoY revenue decline as a public company.

Pickles framed his return around the company’s existing team. “I’m thrilled by all the progress our engineering and product teams have made in recent years,” Pickles said in a statement. “The foundation here is strong, and I’m excited to support the team and help guide them through a new era of rapid change and opportunity.”

Green and Pickles founded The Trade Desk in 2009. Pickles came from AdECN, an ad exchange Microsoft acquired, where he led a team that built its real-time building exchange.

When he stepped down in 2023, he handed day-to-day technical leadership to Sairaj Uddin, then SVP of engineering, and Gruia Pitigoi-Aron, then SVP of product, both reporting to Green.

The Trade Desk’s technology has been at the center of its troubles since then. The adtech company launched the Kokai platform in 2023, around the time Pickles left. ADWEEK reported that advertisers frustrated with Kokai moved millions of dollars in ad spend away from the platform to Amazon, and the company later sunsetted Kokai.

The Trade Desk has also seen turnover in its finance leadership, including a string of CFO departures. More recently it pushed into AI agents with Koa, its conversational AI interface.

The company did not say whether Pickles is returning full time, rejoining the board or holding an officer title, or how the CTO Council will divide authority with the company’s current engineering and product leaders. The Trade Desk declined to comment on those details and pointed to its blog post announcing the move.

Trishla is an Adweek staff reporter covering AI and tech.

Increasingly, the answer is not a person. AI agents, AI assistants, crawlers, headless browsers, and malicious automation now make up a large and fast-growing share of internet traffic, and telling them apart from humans — and from each other — is becoming one of the most important problems our customers ask us to solve.

We are standing up a dedicated Automation Intelligence engineering team to own that problem end to end: detection in the browser, detection at the edge, the classification taxonomy that tells customers what kind of automation they're seeing and how confident we are, and the API that customers and their agents call.

The team has unusual autonomy and unusual visibility. It owns a product that leadership considers strategically critical, it is the first team operating Fingerprint's AI-native engineering model, and its results — good and bad — will be used as evidence for how the rest of engineering works. That means two things: you'll have real room to decide how the team operates, and you'll need to be honest about what the data shows.

In the first 90 days you will likely: finalize the roster and ownership lines with the sibling team, get the quality loop and engineering baselines running, deliver the engineering evidence for the Beta gate, and run the first review of the AI-native operating model.

#7333. Respect Over Authenticity: Whalar Group Rewrites the Brand-Creator Partnership

Ten years ago, brands were still asking why they should be on Instagram. Today, creators sit at the heart of strategy conversations from day one, owning stakes in companies and building businesses that look increasingly like media and entertainment companies in their own right.

That shift has changed what brands should expect from creator partnerships and what creators need to build behind the scenes.

On this episode of The Speed of Culture podcast, Neil Waller, co-founder and co-CEO of Whalar Group, joins Matt Britton to examine how experimentation, a simple thing like mutual respect, and operational rigor are shaping the next phase of the creator economy.

He also breaks down why AI could make trusted tastemakers even more valuable as content becomes increasingly abundant.

Neil Waller is the co-founder and co-CEO of Whalar Group, a creator-focused business that began as an influencer marketing agency in 2016 and has since grown into a six-company creator ecosystem.

Before Whalar, Neil and his co-founder James built their own direct-to-consumer brands on Shopify, using early creator partnerships to generate content and sales. That firsthand experience became the foundation for a business built around helping brands work more effectively with creators and, increasingly, helping creators build lasting companies around their audiences and influence.

[01:34] Proof Came Before the Pitch — Whalar’s creator thesis did not begin with an agency deck. Neil and his co-founder were already using creators to market their own Shopify brands, which gave them firsthand evidence that creator-led content could influence real purchasing behavior. For marketers, the lesson is simple: conviction becomes more credible when it comes from having tested the model yourself.

[02:57] Experimentation Beats Prediction — Neil is quick to reject the idea that Whalar’s early success came from seeing the future perfectly. Their watch brand worked because they could test quickly, learn from the market, and lean harder into what gained traction. Timing helped, but so did being willing to be wrong before becoming right. That mindset became more useful than relying on a rigid business plan in a market that was still taking shape.

[09:12] Respect Matters More Than Authenticity — “Authenticity” has long dominated creator marketing conversations, but Neil argues that mutual respect is the more useful standard. Brands need to understand why audiences trust creators, while creators need to understand what the brand represents and needs to achieve. The strongest collaborations sit between total brand control and total creative freedom.

[10:35] Creators Are The New Business Partners — Creators are no longer being added after the campaign strategy has already been written. Neil sees them increasingly contributing creative ideas, audience insight, and even business value through deeper partnerships and ownership structures. As creators become media and entertainment companies themselves, brands have an opportunity to build relationships that go far beyond sponsored content.

[13:03] Operational Rigor Is Non-Negotiable — Audience connection and great ideas can create momentum, but Neil argues that neither can compensate for weak execution forever. As creator businesses become larger and more complex, the infrastructure around them matters more. Professional teams, clear processes, and disciplined execution are what allow creative companies to turn attention into sustainable businesses.

[16:50] AI Could Make Taste More Valuable — Neil expects AI to dramatically increase the volume of personalized content while simultaneously pushing audiences toward trusted people and brands that can help them decide what is actually worth their attention. In his “barbell” view of content, abundance grows at one end while human taste and curation become more valuable at the other. The middle, generic content without a clear point of view, could become the hardest place to compete.

Matt Britton is America’s leading expert on the millennial generation having consulted for over half of the Fortune 500 over the past two decades. He is the Founder and CEO of Suzy and the host of The Speed of Culture Podcast.

Select ADWEEK as one of your favorite sources and stay up to date on our latest content.

Plus, why doing the right thing beats the short-term number.

Ben & Jerry's cofounder reveals the winner of the brief he set to the marketing industry at Brandweek in September

Imunify360 is CloudLinux's multi-layer security suite for Linux web servers: antivirus and malware cleanup, WAF, IDS/IPS, Proactive Defense, patch management (KernelCare/Patchman), and IP/domain reputation. You'll drive technical decisions, improve architecture, establish engineering standards, and help teams build sustainable solutions.

#7313. Senior Infrastructure Engineer II at Kit

Kit is the email marketing platform experts choose to grow their audience, deepen their relationships, and build a business around the work they love. We're also embracing AI thoughtfully — both in how we build and how we hire to ensure our team is adaptable, innovative, and ready for what's next.

We're looking for a Senior Infrastructure Engineer II to take ownership of a large share of Kit's core infrastructure: databases, Kubernetes, version upgrades, platform tooling, and day-to-day support. You'll also build the paved paths that let engineers, non-engineers, and AI agents test and ship changes safely.

The infrastructure supports creators who depend on Kit to reach their audiences and earn a living. When it's healthy, nobody notices. That's the job.

This is a full-time Individual Contributor role for an experienced infrastructure engineer who likes owning production systems end to end, takes on-call seriously, leans into automation and enjoys making other people's work easier.

You'll report to Mojtaba Hosseini, VP of Engineering at Kit. Day to day, you'll work most closely with Kris Hamoud and Ivan Mora Perez.

You'll also partner with product engineers across Kit, and with teams outside engineering who rely on infrastructure to get their work done. Once you're ramped up, you'll join a three-person on-call rotation, which works out to roughly one week in three.

First Week: Get access to our AWS accounts, Kubernetes clusters, monitoring, and CI/CD pipelines. Read through our runbooks and architecture docs. Take part in Get To Know Yous (GTKYs) with Kris, Ivan, and the engineering teams you'll support.

First Month: Pick up day-to-day infrastructure support requests and start working through them with Kris and Ivan as backup. Ship your first improvement, such as a version upgrade or a fix to a gap in monitoring. Shadow on-call shifts. Write down what you learn along the way, especially the parts that only lived in someone's head or those that can be automated or improved.

First Six Months: Own core infrastructure areas end to end: databases, Kubernetes, version upgrades, and platform tooling. Take your full share of on-call, which you'll start by the end of your first quarter. Become the go-to person for day-to-day infrastructure questions. Find one recurring source of pages and make it go away. Ship a first paved path, such as a sandbox environment where AI agents or non-engineers can safely build and test changes.

First Year: You're the primary owner of a large part of Kit's core infrastructure, and you lead projects in it without needing direction. Uptime stays at 99.9% or better, page volume is down, and you've found real cost savings. Your paved paths are in regular use by engineers, non-engineers, and AI agents. You've built observability good enough for AI agents to handle some on-call load. You've documented your decisions so others can build on your work.

Running production infrastructure on AWS at scale, including managed relational databases, networking, and load balancing

Operating Kubernetes in production: upgrades, capacity planning, and troubleshooting

Managing infrastructure as code and maintaining CI/CD pipelines that engineers trust

Building monitoring and alerting that catches real problems early and doesn't page people for noise

Diagnosing complex, multi-service incidents calmly, then fixing the underlying cause

Cutting cloud costs through right-sizing and reserved capacity without hurting reliability

Writing clear runbooks, docs, and decision write-ups

Nice to have: experience with Cloudflare, ephemeral or sandbox environments, or email sending infrastructure

Around 7 or more years in infrastructure, platform, DevOps, or SRE roles

Owning production infrastructure for a product where downtime costs customers money

Carrying a pager for systems you owned, and making that on-call rotation quieter over time

Leading a major upgrade or migration, such as a database or Kubernetes version upgrade, with little or no customer impact

Building self-service tooling or paved paths that other engineers actually adopted

Working on a small infrastructure team where you couldn't hand problems off to a specialist

You own what you run. You spot infrastructure debt and fix it without waiting to be asked, and you follow through until it's done.

You build quality into the work. Upgrades have rollback plans, maintenance windows are planned around customers, and "good enough" is a decision, not a default.

You leverage AI effectively. AI helps you increase quality and velocity where it matters most. You know when to delegate and when to dive in, while holding accountability.

You make work easier for everyone else. You'd rather build a tool or write a guide once than answer the same question ten times.

You teach what you know. You write things down so the next person, or the next AI agent, can build on your work.

You work in public. You share context and tradeoffs openly, including during incidents and when things go wrong.

You use AI thoughtfully. It's part of how you work, and you help others adopt it without creating new risks.

You remember who's on the other end. An outage means a creator's launch email doesn't go out, and that shapes how you prioritize.

Wanting a well-defined backlog handed to you each week. Here, you'll find much of the work yourself and set priorities with Kris.

Preferring not to be on call. On-call is a core part of this role, shared across a team of three.

You are skeptical of AI’s capabilities, or conversely use AI without good judgement and accountability.

Wanting mostly greenfield architecture. A lot of this work is running, upgrading, and improving systems that already exist.

Needing layers of approval before acting.

We're 100% remote with team members across the globe. We work mostly on East Coast time—so if you're based on the West Coast of the United States, Europe, or Asia, you might have a few early mornings or late evenings. We do our best to keep things reasonable, but a little time zone juggling comes with the territory.

Kit has standardized salaries based on position, no matter where you live. For this role, we're hiring at our level 4 ($197,000). Level is determined based on experience and our interview process.

Four-week, paid sabbatical after five years with the team

#7304. Brandweek 2026: Why Marketing Belongs in Product, Finance, and Tech with Petzey’s Ameetess Dira

In the age of AI, marketers still have to answer one big question: “Why should anyone choose you?”

Live from the Marketing Vanguard stage at Brandweek 2026, Jenny Rooney sits down with Ameetess Dira, CMO of Petzey, for a conversation about the decisions that become even more important as marketing gets easier to automate.

Ameetess breaks down why purpose alignment is a non-negotiable in her career, and why she deliberately pushes marketing into product, technology, and finance conversations.

They also discuss why some of her strongest brand partnerships looked strange on paper.

This episode is part of a special vidcast series that was recorded live during Brandweek 2026 and presented in partnership with Tatari.

Ameetess Dira is CMO at Petzey, an on-demand pet telehealth app with more than 1,000 pet professionals across the U.S.

Her path into marketing has crossed broadcasting, radio, print, agency leadership, technology, and international market expansion across Asia and the United States.

Her move into pet health was deeply personal. After losing her senior dog, Ameetess deliberately sought out work that could improve access to care for animals and the people who love them. That purpose now shapes how she thinks about everything from partnerships and crisis response to the role marketing should play inside the wider business.

Purpose Alignment Is Not a Nice-to-Have — For Ameetess, belief in the company and its leadership is a non-negotiable requirement. She wants to work with people she trusts enough to do difficult things with because the work serves something bigger than the individuals involved. She makes the distinction clear: purpose is not a layer applied to marketing after the strategy is done. It is the filter that determines whether she wants to do the work in the first place.

Look Where Everyone Else Says “That’s Too Small” — Senior dog adoption is difficult, expensive, and easy for brands to ignore; that is exactly why Ameetess pays attention to it. Her approach is to look at how culture is changing, where people remain underserved and where a brand can contribute something useful instead of competing for the same obvious audience as everyone else.

Marketing Has No Business Staying in Marketing — Ameetess deliberately inserts marketing into conversations with product, technology, and finance. Her reasoning is simple – the executive team is her primary team, and everyone around that table is responsible for the overall health of the business. The CMO who stays inside campaigns and communications is giving up influence before the real decisions are even made.

Sometimes the Right Decision Is the One the Spreadsheet Hates — During the LA fires, Ameetess opened Petzey’s platform for free wellness checks for people affected by the crisis, including first responders, teachers, businesses, and shelters. The short-term financial logic pointed toward monetizing demand, and she still chose the opposite. Her argument is that doing the right thing can matter more than hitting the immediate number, especially when the decision proves what the brand actually stands for.

Jenny Rooney is Chief Brand and Community Officer, leading strategy for the overall ADWEEK brand as well as the ways in which we serve and support our audiences with high-value content, products, partnerships and experiences, notably through our community programs such as Marketing Vanguard.

Select ADWEEK as one of your favorite sources and stay up to date on our latest content.

The Liquid Death and Ibotta executives on optimizing promotional spend across retail channels and reducing customer churn.

Ben & Jerry's cofounder reveals the winner of the brief he set to the marketing industry at Brandweek in September

At Salesloft, our People Operations Coordinator is foundational to a high-performing People team. Each coordinator embeds with their assigned business function(s) (e.g., Engineering, Marketing, Sales) to provide full employee lifecycle support, from offer through exit. As the first point of contact for employees and managers, coordinators deliver high-volume, high-accuracy service across HR transactions, data, leave, employee relations support, and employee questions, creating a consistently excellent employee experience. They partner closely with People Business Partners, keeping the work moving, the data clean, and the PBP fully informed, and work alongside Total Rewards, Benefits and Mobility, and Talent Acquisition so programs run smoothly.

Each coordinator also serves as the expert for an assigned country, bringing local compliance knowledge, language, and time zone coverage to the team. Working within a shared services model, coordinators cover for one another across functions during peak periods and absences, and bring back improvements that strengthen the whole team. This is a broad, high-visibility role and a clear path toward People Business Partner or People Operations leadership.

About 60 - 70% of your time supports your assigned business function(s) and their People Business Partner. That includes lifecycle transactions, employee data, questions, ER support, and the people programs for your pod.

The remaining 30–40% supports the broader People team. You will help Total Rewards with compensation and benefits cycles, partner with People Systems on HiBob, data, and reporting, and support Talent Acquisition through the handoff from offer to onboarding. You’ll also serve as the expert for your assigned country and cover for teammates during peak periods.

The balance shifts with the business calendar. Expect more Total Rewards work during open enrollment and compensation cycles, for example. We review it regularly throughout the year.

This role begins as a three-month contract with the opportunity to convert to a full-time, permanent Salesloft position based on business needs and performance. The contract period gives you the chance to learn the team, the systems and the business function you support, and gives us the chance to work together before making a long-term commitment. We will check in with you along the way so you know how things are going.

We are looking for a service-minded, detail-obsessed People professional who takes pride in getting the work right the first time. You understand that every offer letter, record update, and employee question is someone’s experience of the company, and you treat each one that way. You are comfortable being the first point of contact for employees and managers, and you know when to resolve a request yourself and when to bring in a People Business Partner or specialist. You handle confidential information with discretion and communicate clearly in writing. You are equally at home working through a high volume of transactions and spotting the pattern behind them, and you bring ideas back that make the whole team better. You enjoy partnering with business leaders, adapt easily across time zones and cultures, and want to build toward a career as a People Business Partner or in People Operations leadership.

Our People Operations Coordinators, PBPs and specialists work as one team on a shared goal, and each role has clear ownership. Coordinators are the front door of the People organization: warm, responsive and exact, with the pride in getting it right that earns managers’ and employees’ trust. We are a global team across India, Mexico and the United States, we cover for one another, and we share what we learn so every pod gets better. Benefits include a generous healthcare package, 401k matching program, catered lunches, and flexible vacation time. Our headquarters are located in the Mission District of San Francisco, CA. We're hiring for a mix of remote roles and hybrid roles.  For those who are remote, we have a handful of opportunities throughout the year for in person collaboration.  For our hybrid roles, we're flexible, and you're an adult—we don't expect or mandate that you're in the office every day. We do believe there are valuable and serendipitous moments of discovery and collaboration that come from working together in person.

Descript is an equal opportunity workplace—we are dedicated to equal employment opportunities regardless of race, color, ancestry, religion, sex, national origin, sexual orientation, age, citizenship, marital status, disability, gender identity, or Veteran status. With built-in governance, auditability, and human oversight, Camunda gives enterprises the control they need to move AI from pilots to production — safely and at scale. Trusted by over 700 organizations worldwide, including 9 of top 10 US banks, Camunda helps enterprises boost operational efficiency, accelerate time-to-value, and deliver better customer experiences.Fully remote and global, we are in the middle of something bigger: transforming into an AI-first organisation, built on our own platform. We use Agentic AI to automate, orchestrate intelligent processes, and elevate human contribution across every team.Named GP Bullhound’s Top 100 Next Unicorn list, 2025 Great Place to Work certified. Visionary in 2025 Gartner® Magic Quadrant™ for Business Orchestration and Automation Technologies. ranked 3rd in Flexa's 2026 Most Flexible Companies, We’re growing fast and looking for top talent to join our team. If you want meaningful work, visible impact and put something genuinely rare on your CV, keep reading.

As a Senior Software Engineer, Backend on Camunda’s Distributed Systems team, you’ll help build and evolve the durable, highly available, horizontally scalable streaming platform that underpins Camunda 8’s Orchestration Cluster and powers mission‑critical processes for customers around the world.

As part of the Distributed Systems team, you will work on technically challenging and rewarding projects, such as expanding on our multi-Raft implementation, our SWIM-based cluster membership algorithm, or high performance network and disk I/O pipelines.

At Camunda, we approach engineering in a holistic manner. As such, you will form strong partnerships with product management, design, QA, and sales, and work with your peers to not only deliver but directly influence the evolution of our platform to meet our customer’s needs.

Curious about the kind of challenges you'll work on at Camunda? Watch this quick 30-minute talk from our engineers to learn more about the new Camunda Exporter and how we’re solving complex problems at scale

Design, build, and maintain backend services for the distributed streaming platform that powers the foundation for Camunda 8’s orchestration capabilities, ensuring durability, high availability, and horizontal scalability.

Lead medium‑to‑large engineering initiatives (3+ months) end‑to‑end: partner with Product to translate business problems into technical requirements, write solution designs, draft and maintain project plans, and drive timely, high‑quality delivery.

Help mature our disaster recovery and resilience story, including implementing and evolving an online backup/restore API and contributing to improved multi‑region and multi‑tenant capabilities for our cloud offering.

Design and implement developer and operator tooling that makes it easier to understand, troubleshoot, and safely operate a complex distributed system in production environments.

Improve engineering effectiveness by collaborating with DevOps and other Core Application teams on CI, testing, and local development workflows to reduce PR time‑to‑merge and the overhead of working on multiple features in parallel.

Participate in L3 support and on‑call rotations, partnering closely with Support and SRE to investigate and resolve complex, performance‑sensitive production issues, and feeding what you learn back into our product and platform.

7+ years of professional experience as a software engineer, with a strong focus on backend systems and/or distributed systems.

Proven experience leading medium‑to‑large engineering initiatives (roughly 3+ months), breaking down ambiguous problems, writing clear solution designs and project plans, coordinating with multiple teams, and driving predictable delivery.

Hands‑on experience building and maintaining highly concurrent, data‑intensive software, with a deep understanding of low‑level network and disk I/O (for example, throughput, latency, backpressure, batching, and failure modes).

Demonstrated ability to work autonomously and navigate ambiguity – you are comfortable taking on problems you haven’t solved before, breaking them down into concrete steps, and driving them to completion with minimal supervision.

Strong communication and collaboration skills in a remote‑first environment: you tailor explanations to your audience, translate business requirements into actionable technical work with Product, and help teams reach clear, decisive outcomes with minimal churn.

Experience building workflow automation or process orchestration software.

Experience with distributed systems algorithms or protocols such as Raft, Paxos, SWIM, and/or CRDTs.

Strong familiarity with the observability pillars (logging, metrics, tracing) and how to integrate observability into the development and incident lifecycle, ideally including experience with L3 support and on‑call.

Deep knowledge of the Java Virtual Machine, especially its memory model and performance characteristics in highly concurrent, data‑intensive workloads.
